```text
      <div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true. What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff. This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p> <p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things,
```

```text
no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p> <p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records. From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p> <p>&nbsp;</p> <h3>Streams interact with groundwater in all types of landscapes</h3> <p>The interaction takes place in three basic ways:</p> <ul>
```

**[14]** 1942c | quality 75.0 | ⚠ trailing_heading_orphan, oversized_chars
```text
<div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>
</div>
</div>
<div class="field field--name--field-content field--type--entity-reference-revisions field--label--hidden">

<h3>Rivers Contain Groundwater</h3>
<p><strong>Water in streams contains groundwater</strong></p>
<figure role="group" class="align-right side-image">
<div class="d-media embedded-media d-media--type--image media--view-mode--side-image">
```

```text
Credit: R.A. McNish, USGS</figcaption>
</figure>
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true. What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff.
```
**[16]** 1208c | quality 85.0 | ⚠ trailing_heading_orphan
```text
The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff. This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records. From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
```

```text
<div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>

</div>

</div>

<div class="field field--name--field-content field--type--entity-reference-revisions field--label--hidden">
```


```text
<h3>Rivers Contain Groundwater</h3>
<p><strong>Water in streams contains groundwater</strong></p>
<figure role="group" class="align-right side-image">
```

```text
<figcaption>Groundwater discharge from springs in the Redwall Limestone wall of the Grand Canyon cascades into the Colorado River at Vasey's Paradise in the Grand Canyon.<br>
<br>
Credit: R.A. McNish, USGS</figcaption>
</figure>
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true.
```
**[67]** 413c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff.
```
**[68]** 300c | quality 70.0 | ⚠ dangling_pronoun
```text
This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
```
**[69]** 518c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records.
```
**[70]** 415c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<h3>Streams interact with groundwater in all types of landscapes</h3>
<p>The interaction takes place in three basic ways:</p>
<ul>
```

```text
[Document Topic: </div> <button class="mobile-nav-close"> <span class="usa-sr-only">Close</span> </button> </div> <div…]
<div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>

</div>

</div>

<div class="field field--name--field-content field--type--entity-reference-revisions field--label--hidden">
```

```text
<h3>Rivers Contain Groundwater</h3>
<p><strong>Water in streams contains groundwater</strong></p>
<figure role="group" class="align-right side-image">
```

```text
[Document Topic: </div> <button class="mobile-nav-close"> <span class="usa-sr-only">Close</span> </button> </div> <div…]
<figcaption>Groundwater discharge from springs in the Redwall Limestone wall of the Grand Canyon cascades into the Colorado River at Vasey's Paradise in the Grand Canyon.<br>
<br>
Credit: R.A. McNish, USGS</figcaption>
</figure>
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true.
```
**[67]** 534c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
[Document Topic: </div> <button class="mobile-nav-close"> <span class="usa-sr-only">Close</span> </button> </div> <div…]
What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff.
```
**[68]** 421c | quality 92.0 | ⚠ dangling_pronoun_mitigated_by_prefix
```text
[Document Topic: </div> <button class="mobile-nav-close"> <span class="usa-sr-only">Close</span> </button> </div> <div…]
This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
```
**[69]** 639c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
[Document Topic: </div> <button class="mobile-nav-close"> <span class="usa-sr-only">Close</span> </button> </div> <div…]
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records.
```

```text
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<h3>Streams interact with groundwater in all types of landscapes</h3>
<p>The interaction takes place in three basic ways:</p>
```

```text
<div about="/special-topics/water-science-school/science/rivers-contain-groundwater" class="node node--type--science node--view-mode--overview grid-row grid-gap-3">

  <div class="node-main desktop:grid-col-12">

          
<div class="intro-cta">
      
<div class="field field--name--field-intro field--type--text-long field--label--hidden">
    
      <div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>
  
</div>

  </div>

<div class="field field--name--field-content field--type--entity-reference-revisions field--label--hidden">
  
          <div class="field-content field-loop-count-1 field-first field-last">  <div class="paragraph paragraph--type--wysiwyg-text-with-media paragraph--view-mode--default">
          
<div class="field field--name--field-text-with-media field--type--text-long field--label--hidden">
    
      <div class="field-text-with-media"><div class="tex2jax_process"><p><strong>•&nbsp;&nbsp;<a href="/special-topic/water-science-school">Water Science School HOME</a>&nbsp;&nbsp;•&nbsp;&nbsp;<a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-topics">Groundwater topics</a>&nbsp; •&nbsp; <a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-water-topics-0">Surface Water topics</a>&nbsp; •</strong></p>
<h3>Rivers Contain Groundwater</h3>
<p><strong>Water in streams contains groundwater</strong></p>
<figure role="group" class="align-right side-image">

  <div class="d-media embedded-media d-media--type--image media--view-mode--side-image">
          <a href="/media/images/groundwater-flow-or-discharge-water-emerges-cuts-rock-layers">
            
<div class="field field--name--image field--type--image field--label--hidden">
    
      <div class="image">  <img loading="lazy" src="https://d9-wret.s3.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/assets/palladium/production/s3fs-public/styles/side_image/public/thumbnails/image/wss-gw-discharge-from-canyon.jpg?itok=Tjs31SbE" width="340" height="240" alt="Groundwater flowing from cracks in a wall of the Grand Canyon." typeof="foaf:Image">


</div>
  
</div>

          </a>
              <div class="d-media-copyright">
        Sources/Usage: Public Domain. <a href="/media/images/groundwater-flow-or-discharge-water-emerges-cuts-rock-layers">View Media Details</a>
      </div>
          </div>

<figcaption>Groundwater discharge from springs in the Redwall Limestone wall of the Grand Canyon cascades into the Colorado River at Vasey's Paradise in the Grand Canyon.<br>
<br>
Credit: R.A. McNish, USGS</figcaption>
</figure>
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true. What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff. This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records. From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<h3>Streams interact with groundwater in all types of landscapes</h3>
<p>The interaction takes place in three basic ways:</p>
```

```text
<div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river.
```

**[33]** 629c | quality 90.0 | ⚠ starts_with_connector
```text
But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>
```

```text
<h3>Rivers Contain Groundwater</h3>
<p><strong>Water in streams contains groundwater</strong></p>
<figure role="group" class="align-right side-image">
<div class="d-media embedded-media d-media--type--image media--view-mode--side-image">
```

**[36]** 416c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true.
```
**[37]** 413c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff.
```
**[38]** 399c | quality 70.0 | ⚠ dangling_pronoun
```text
This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world.
```
**[39]** 419c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records.
```
**[40]** 415c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<h3>Streams interact with groundwater in all types of landscapes</h3>
<p>The interaction takes place in three basic ways:</p>
```

```text
<div class="field field--name--field-intro field--type--text-long field--label--hidden">
<div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>
```
```text
</div>
```

```text
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true.
```
**[270]** 830c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true. What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff.
```
**[271]** 813c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff. This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world.
```
**[272]** 1090c | quality 70.0 | ⚠ dangling_pronoun
```text
This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records. From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
```
**[273]** 774c | quality 85.0 | ⚠ trailing_heading_orphan
```text
Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records. From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
```

```text
<div class="field-intro"><p>Naturally, the water running in rivers comes from precipitation that runs off the landscape into the river. But since precipitation also seeps into (and moves) into the ground, you don't often consider that a significant amount of the water flowing in rivers comes from water in the ground seeping back "up" into the river from below.&nbsp;</p></div>
</div>
</div>
```

**[95]** 1141c | quality 100.0 | ✅
```text
</figure>
<p>It is probably a common conception that the water flowing in rivers and streams comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/rain-and-precipitation">precipitation</a></strong> <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/runoff-surface-and-overland-water-runoff">runoff</a></strong> from the landscape into the river. Of course that is true, but it is not entirely true. What I mean is, <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-what-groundwater">groundwater</a></strong> contributes to streams in most physiographic and climatic settings to a certain degree; some of the water flowing in rivers comes from seepage of groundwater into the streambed. The water flowing in rivers still originates from precipitation, but it is not all from surface runoff. This <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/groundwater-flows-underground">groundwater seepage</a></strong> is vitally important to the hydrologic settings of the world because it is responsible for keeping water in rivers during times of no rainfall (base flow conditions).</p>
```
**[96]** 803c | quality 85.0 | ⚠ trailing_heading_orphan
```text
<p>Groundwater contributes to streamflow in most physiographic and climatic settings in the world. Many things, such as a region's geography, geology, soil type and saturation, and climate, determine the proportion of stream water that comes from groundwater. <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/what-hydrology">Hydrologists</a></strong> (water scientists) can determine the amount of water that groundwater contributes to streams by analyzing streamflow hydrographs and precipitation records. From these studies, hydrologists can determine how much of a stream's flow comes from <strong><a href="/special-topic/water-science-school/science/surface-runoff-and-water-cycle">runoff</a></strong> and how much comes from groundwater discharging into the streambed.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
```
